Are you feeling sad, worried, frustrated, or something else?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure. I don't think I feel sad, exactly. Just... blank, I suppose. I haven't really thought about the emotional part of it.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year or so, have there been times when you felt sad, down, or depressed—even if just for a few hours at a time?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really think so. Not sad. Just... tired, maybe. But no, not depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory, or do you ever feel confused about things that you normally wouldn't be confused about?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't noticed anything like that. My memory is... well, it's fine, I suppose. I don't really get confused.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that table unable to decide what to do first, do you notice your heart racing, or do you feel tense or worried about things in general—or is it more that you just don't feel motivated or interested in doing anything?\n\nPatient: No... my heart doesn't race. I don't feel worried, really. And it's not that I'm not interested in doing things... I just... sit there. I don't really know how to explain it.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do a task—like paying a bill or making a phone call—do you find it takes you longer to think through the steps than it used to, or do the words come slower when you're talking?\n\nPatient: No... I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your husband started needing more help—about a year and three months ago—did you have any illness, surgery, or start taking any new medications? Or has a doctor mentioned any changes in your physical health?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I haven't been sick. I just take my usual pills. Nothing's really changed with my health.\n\nDoctor: When your husband started needing more help with his appointments and daily arrangements, how did that make you feel at the time? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I suppose it's been this much of a problem for... about a year and three months. Give or take. It really started piling up when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments... and his daily arrangements. Just... since then, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that table looking at all those things, how do you feel emotionally? Are you feeling sad, worried, frustrated, or something else?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure. I don't think I feel sad, exactly. Just... blank, I suppose. I haven't really thought about the emotional part of it.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year or so, have there been times when you felt sad, down, or depressed—even if just for a few hours at a time?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really think so. Not sad. Just... tired, maybe. But no, not depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory, or do you ever feel confused about things that you normally wouldn't be confused about?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't noticed anything like that. My memory is... well, it's fine, I suppose. I don't really get confused.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that table unable to decide what to do first, do you notice your heart racing, or do you feel tense or worried about things in general—or is it more that you just don't feel motivated or interested in doing anything?\n\nPatient: No... my heart doesn't race. I don't feel worried, really. And it's not that I'm not interested in doing things... I just... sit there. I don't really know how to explain it.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do a task—like paying a bill or making a phone call—do you find it takes you longer to think through the steps than it used to, or do the words come slower when you're talking?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think it takes longer to think through the steps. And I don't think my words are slower. I just... I don't really do the tasks. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your husband started needing more help—about a year and three months ago—did you have any illness, surgery, or start taking any new medications? Or has a doctor mentioned any changes in your physical health?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I haven't been sick. I just take my usual pills. Nothing's really changed with my health.\n\nDoctor: When your husband started needing more help with his appointments and daily arrangements, how did that make you feel at the time? Did it feel overwhelming, or did something shift in how you felt about things in general?\n\nPatient: I don't know. It was just... something that had to be done. I didn't really feel much about it. It just is what it is.\n\nDoctor: Before this year and three months ago, were there things you enjoyed doing—hobbies, activities, time with friends or family—that you looked forward to? And if so, do you still feel that enjoyment when you do those things now, or has that changed?\n\nPatient: I used to knit a bit... maybe read some. I don't really do much of that anymore. I just... haven't felt like it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about knitting or reading now, if you were to try it, do you think you would enjoy it like you used to, or does the idea of doing it just not appeal to you at all?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I suppose it just doesn't appeal to me. I haven't really thought about trying.\n\nDoctor: If you sat down right now and started knitting or reading, do you think you would feel some enjoyment or pleasure while doing it, or would it feel flat and unrewarding even while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure. I haven't tried. It just... probably wouldn't feel like much of anything.\n\nDoctor: If I asked you to do something you used to enjoy—like knitting—and you actually did it, would you find it pleasant and rewarding in the moment, or would it feel empty and pointless even while you were doing it?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I just... I don't know how it would feel. It's been a while, so... I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: If your daughter or a close friend asked you to go knitting with them this afternoon and you actually went, do you think you would feel some enjoyment or pleasure while you were doing it together?\n\nPatient: I suppose... seeing my daughter would be fine. But as for the knitting itself... I really just don't know. I can't say.\n\nDoctor: If you were knitting right now with your daughter sitting next to you, and she was enjoying it and talking with you about it, do you think you would feel any pleasure or satisfaction from the activity itself in that moment?\n\nPatient: I don't know why we keep going over this. I just... I can't predict how I would feel. It's too hard to guess. I really don't have a better answer for you.\n\nDoctor: If you were to do something right now that you know objectively is good or pleasant—like eating your favorite food or receiving good news—would you actually feel happy or pleased about it, or would it feel kind of numb and not really matter to you?\n\nPatient: I... I really couldn't say. I just... I don't know how I'd react. It's hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you do manage to do something—like when your husband asks you for help with his appointments and you actually help him—do you feel satisfied or glad that you helped him, or does helping him feel like it doesn't matter one way or the other?\n\nPatient: I just... I don't really pay attention to that. If he needs help, I just try to do it. I couldn't tell you if I feel glad about it... I'm really not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you do help your husband with his appointments, even though you're doing it, do you feel any sense of accomplishment or satisfaction from completing the task itself—or does it feel completely neutral and empty, like it doesn't register as good or bad?\n\nPatient: I... I just don't know. I do it because he asks. I don't really think about how it feels. It's just a thing that happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
